SELECT n.n_name,
       SUM(l.l_extendedprice * (1 - l.l_discount)) AS revenue
FROM region AS r
JOIN nation AS n ON n.n_regionkey = r.r_regionkey
JOIN supplier AS s ON s.s_nationkey = n.n_nationkey
JOIN customer AS c ON c.c_nationkey = s.s_nationkey

  AND o.o_orderdate < DATE '1994-01-01'

```sql
WITH america_nations AS (
    SELECT n.n_nationkey,
           n.n_name
    FROM nation n
    JOIN region r
      ON n.n_regionkey = r.r_regionkey
    WHERE r.r_name = 'AMERICA'
),
orders_1993 AS (
    SELECT o.o_orderkey,
           o.o_custkey
    FROM orders o
    WHERE o.o_orderdate >= DATE '1993-01-01'
      AND o.o_orderdate <  DATE '1994-01-01'
)
SELECT an.n_name,
       SUM(l.l_extendedprice * (1 - l.l_discount)) AS revenue
FROM orders_1993 o
JOIN lineitem l
  ON l.l_orderkey = o.o_orderkey
JOIN supplier s
  ON s.s_suppkey = l.l_suppkey
JOIN america_nations an
  ON an.n_nationkey = s.s_nationkey
JOIN customer c
  ON c.c_custkey = o.o_custkey
 AND c.c_nationkey = an.n_nationkey
GROUP BY an.n_name
ORDER BY revenue DESC;
```

```sql
SELECT n.n_name,
       SUM(l.l_extendedprice * (1 - l.l_discount)) AS revenue
FROM orders AS o
JOIN customer AS c
  ON c.c_custkey = o.o_custkey
JOIN lineitem AS l
  ON l.l_orderkey = o.o_orderkey
JOIN supplier AS s
  ON s.s_suppkey = l.l_suppkey
 AND s.s_nationkey = c.c_nationkey
JOIN nation AS n
  ON n.n_nationkey = s.s_nationkey
JOIN region AS r
  ON r.r_regionkey = n.n_regionkey
WHERE o.o_orderdate >= DATE '1993-01-01'
  AND o.o_orderdate < DATE '1994-01-01'
  AND r.r_name = 'AMERICA'
GROUP BY n.n_name
ORDER BY revenue DESC;
```

</analysis>
The rewritten query preserves all original semantics: the same join keys, the same nation-equality condition between customer and supplier, the AMERICA region filter, and the one-year orders date range. The main textual improvements are explicit JOIN syntax, moving the c_nationkey = s_nationkey condition into the supplier join, and replacing the interval expression with a direct date literal.

Given the data sizes, the dominant cost is lineitem (59,986,052 rows). The most important practical optimizations beyond SQL text are physical:
- Index orders(o_orderdate) INCLUDE (o_orderkey, o_custkey) to cheaply retrieve the one-year orders.
- Index lineitem(l_orderkey) INCLUDE (l_suppkey, l_extendedprice, l_discount) to support joining lineitem by order key without a full scan.
- Optionally, lineitem(l_suppkey, l_orderkey) if the optimizer chooses a supplier-driven plan.
- Ensure primary keys exist on customer(c_custkey) and supplier(s_suppkey), and run ANALYZE on the involved tables.

A CTE is intentionally avoided because there is no redundant computation, and pre-materializing supplier-customer pairs by nation would explode the row count. The rewritten SQL is already clean and equivalent; further improvement should mainly come from indexing and statistics rather than additional query restructuring.
</analysis>

</enhanced_sql>
```sql
SELECT n.n_name,
       SUM(l.l_extendedprice * (1 - l.l_discount)) AS revenue
FROM orders AS o
JOIN customer AS c
  ON c.c_custkey = o.o_custkey
JOIN lineitem AS l
  ON l.l_orderkey = o.o_orderkey
JOIN supplier AS s
  ON s.s_suppkey = l.l_suppkey
 AND s.s_nationkey = c.c_nationkey
JOIN nation AS n
  ON n.n_nationkey = s.s_nationkey
JOIN region AS r
  ON r.r_regionkey = n.n_regionkey
WHERE o.o_orderdate >= DATE '1993-01-01'
  AND o.o_orderdate < DATE '1994-01-01'
  AND r.r_name = 'AMERICA'
GROUP BY n.n_name
ORDER BY revenue DESC;
```

Upon comparing the three EXPLAIN outputs, the key observation is that all three plans are structurally identical: the rewritten plan and the enhanced plan preserve the exact same join tree, scan methods, aggregation strategy, and parallelism as the original plan. The differences are limited to (1) marginal cost reductions of 0.10 cost units at both the top-level Sort node and the underlying Finalize GroupAggregate node, (2) cosmetic alias shortening (e.g., lineitem → l, orders → o), and (3) a normalized date comparison predicate on the orders filter, where the original compared o_orderdate against a timestamp without time zone ('1994-01-01 00:00:00'::timestamp) while the rewritten/enhanced plans compare against a plain date ('1994-01-01'::date). This removes an implicit type cast at execution time.

Cost-wise, the top-level total cost changed from 2167378.47 to 2167378.37, a decrease of 0.10 units, which represents roughly a 0.0000046% improvement — essentially negligible. The same 0.10-unit decrease propagates consistently through Finalize GroupAggregate, Gather Merge, Partial GroupAggregate, and the inner Sort node, indicating the saving originates from the small planning-time constant associated with the eliminated date-type cast expression, not from any access-path change.

Because the plan shape is unchanged, scan types (Parallel Seq Scan on lineitem, supplier, orders, customer; Seq Scan on nation and region), join algorithms (a chain of Parallel Hash Joins plus small inner Hash Joins), aggregation strategy (Partial GroupAggregate → Gather Merge → Finalize GroupAggregate), sort nodes, parallel worker count (2 workers), and all row estimates (with only a trivial 30408 → 30407 adjustment in estimated join cardinality) remain identical between the original and the optimized variants. Memory consumption in Hash nodes, buffer usage patterns, and parallelism are therefore unchanged as well.

In summary, the rewrite/enhancement yielded a semantically equivalent plan with a micro-scale cost reduction driven by predicate normalization, but no meaningful structural, resource, or performance improvement.

1. Cost Efficiency
- Overall cost change: The total plan cost decreased marginally from 2167378.47 to 2167378.37, a reduction of 0.10 cost units (approximately 0.0000046%). This is a negligible improvement and does not indicate any substantive optimization.
- Cost change at the most expensive plan node: The most expensive node is the Parallel Seq Scan on lineitem (cost 1375081.30 in all three plans), which is completely unchanged. The cost reductions of 0.10 units appear uniformly at the Sort, Finalize GroupAggregate, Gather Merge, Partial GroupAggregate, and inner Sort nodes, suggesting the saving stems from a cheaper expression evaluation (the removal of a date-to-timestamp cast) rather than from any access path optimization.

2. Plan Characteristics
- Scan type transitions: None. All three plans use identical scan methods — Parallel Seq Scan on lineitem, supplier, orders, and customer, and standard Seq Scan on nation and region. No transition to Index Scan or Index Only Scan occurred, likely because the queries are large-fact-table aggregations where full scans with parallelism remain optimal.
- Join algorithm refinements: None. The join structure is preserved exactly: a bottom-up chain of Parallel Hash Joins (supplier-nation-region → lineitem → orders → customer), with the outermost join using a two-column composite Hash Cond. No switch to Merge Join, Nested Loop, or any reordering of the join tree took place.
- Elimination of explicit sorting and reduction of intermediate result sets: No change. Both plans still retain two Sort nodes (the inner Sort on n_name feeding Partial GroupAggregate, and the top-level Sort on revenue descending). The estimated intermediate result set changed only trivially from 30408 rows to 30407 rows. No sorting was eliminated and no intermediate result reduction of significance was achieved.

3. Resource Utilization
- Memory usage: Unchanged. All Hash and Parallel Hash nodes retain the same build costs and estimated row counts (e.g., orders Parallel Hash at 956367 rows, customer Parallel Hash at 625000 rows), implying identical hash table memory footprints.
- Worker count: Unchanged. All three plans use 2 planned parallel workers with the same Gather Merge partial aggregation strategy.

4. Other Improvements
- Predicate type normalization: The orders filter changed from comparing o_orderdate < '1994-01-01 00:00:00'::timestamp without time zone to o_orderdate < '1994-01-01'::date. This eliminates a per-row implicit cast during scan evaluation, which is the sole source of the 0.10-unit cost reduction. It slightly improves evaluation efficiency and plan readability, though its runtime impact is expected to be minimal given the scan is dominated by I/O.
- Alias simplification (lineitem → l, nation → n, etc.): purely cosmetic, with no effect on execution.
- Cardinality estimate refinement: the outer join row estimate changed from 30408 to 30407, an inconsequential planner estimate adjustment.
- Overall conclusion: the rewrite and enhancement produced a semantically equivalent, structurally identical plan with only micro-scale predicate-level improvements. For meaningful gains on this workload, further optimization should focus on index creation (e.g., on orders.o_orderdate), materialized views for the nation-level revenue aggregation, or partitioning of the lineitem table rather than query rewriting alone.

Since `c.c_nationkey = an.n_nationkey` AND `s.s_nationkey = an.n_nationkey` transitively imply `c.c_nationkey = s.s_nationkey`, the explicit customer-supplier nation comparison becomes redundant. This preserves result equivalence, eliminates an unnecessary join between customer and supplier, and removes redundant predicate evaluation, shrinking the join search space."

Let's draft:

**Bottlenecks**
- `lineitem` full seq scan (~60M) dominates; hash join with filtered supplier; no lineitem index access.
- `orders` date filter is good but appears as seq scan/filter; date expression can be constant-folded.
- `customer` full seq scan (1.5M) and only later filtered by nation; transitive predicate allows prefiltering to AMERICA.
- Implicit joins obscure join graph; CTE previous rewrite inlined and didn't change plan.

**Strategies and rewrite scores**
- S1 Constant-fold date range: 2/10.
- S2 Explicit ANSI joins + CTE for America nations: 3/10 readability; plan likely same.
- S3 Pre-filter customer and supplier to AMERICA via transitive predicate: 6/10; reduces dimension hash inputs but extra join; test.
- S4 Force materialization of filtered dimension CTEs (PostgreSQL `MATERIALIZED`): 5/10; only if EXPLAIN shows full scans; can be worse.
- S5 Index/covering index strategy: 9/10; most effective for lineitem/orders; e.g. `orders(o_orderdate, o_orderkey, o_custkey)`, `lineitem(l_orderkey) INCLUDE (l_suppkey, l_extendedprice, l_discount)`, `lineitem(l_suppkey) INCLUDE (...)`, `customer(c_nationkey, c_custkey)`, `supplier(s_nationkey, s_suppkey)`. Note existing PK indexes may cover keys.
Most efficient: combine S1/S2/S3 rewrite for clarity and early filtering; run with indexes S5 and verify via EXPLAIN ANALYZE. Do not force CTE materialization unless it improves plan.

**Rewritten SQL** (PostgreSQL, explicit joins, prefilter dimensions):
```sql
WITH america_nations AS (
    SELECT n.n_nationkey, n.n_name
    FROM nation n
    JOIN region r ON n.n_regionkey = r.r_regionkey
    WHERE r.r_name = 'AMERICA'
),
america_customers AS (
    SELECT c.c_custkey, c.c_nationkey
    FROM customer c
    JOIN america_nations an ON c.c_nationkey = an.n_nationkey
),
america_suppliers AS (
    SELECT s.s_suppkey, s.s_nationkey
    FROM supplier s
    JOIN america_nations an ON s.s_nationkey = an.n_nationkey
)
SELECT an.n_name,
       SUM(l.l_extendedprice * (1 - l.l_discount)) AS revenue
FROM orders o
JOIN lineitem l ON l.l_orderkey = o.o_orderkey
JOIN america_suppliers s ON s.s_suppkey = l.l_suppkey
JOIN america_customers c ON c.c_custkey = o.o_custkey
                         AND c.c_nationkey = s.s_nationkey
JOIN america_nations an ON an.n_nationkey = s.s_nationkey
WHERE o.o_orderdate >= DATE '1993-01-01'
  AND o.o_orderdate <  DATE '1994-01-01'
GROUP BY an.n_name
ORDER BY revenue DESC;
```

```sql
select n_name, sum(l_extendedprice * (1 - l_discount)) as revenue
from customer, orders, lineitem, supplier, nation, region
where c_custkey = o_custkey
  and l_orderkey = o_orderkey
  and l_suppkey = s_suppkey
  and c_nationkey = s_nationkey
  and s_nationkey = n_nationkey
  and n_regionkey = r_regionkey
  and r_name = 'AMERICA'
  and o_orderdate >= date '1993-01-01'
  and o_orderdate < date '1994-01-01'
group by n_name
order by revenue desc;
```

```sql
WITH america_nations AS (
    SELECT n.n_nationkey, n.n_name
    FROM nation n
    JOIN region r ON n.n_regionkey = r.r_regionkey
    WHERE r.r_name = 'AMERICA'
)
SELECT an.n_name,
       SUM(l.l_extendedprice * (1 - l.l_discount)) AS revenue
FROM orders o
JOIN lineitem l ON l.l_orderkey = o.o_orderkey
JOIN supplier s ON s.s_suppkey = l.l_suppkey
JOIN america_nations an ON an.n_nationkey = s.s_nationkey
JOIN customer c ON c.c_custkey = o.o_custkey
                AND c.c_nationkey = an.n_nationkey
WHERE o.o_orderdate >= DATE '1993-01-01'
  AND o.o_orderdate <  DATE '1994-01-01'
GROUP BY an.n_name
ORDER BY revenue DESC;
```

</analysis>
The original query is TPC-H Q5. The explain plan shows the cost is dominated by a parallel sequential scan of lineitem (~60M rows) and hash joins; customer (1.5M rows) is also fully scanned and only filtered late via the c_nationkey = s_nationkey condition. Using the transitive equality chain (c_nationkey = s_nationkey = n_nationkey, n in AMERICA), both customer and supplier can be logically restricted to AMERICA nations. The rewrite uses an explicit ANSI JOIN structure and a small nation/region CTE for clarity and early dimension filtering. The date expression is constant-folded to `DATE '1994-01-01'`. The planner may still inline the CTE, so the most decisive performance improvement is the index strategy: orders(o_orderdate) with included (o_orderkey, o_custkey) and a covering index on lineitem(l_orderkey) INCLUDE (l_suppkey, l_extendedprice, l_discount) (or lineitem(l_suppkey)). Avoid forcing MATERIALIZED CTEs unless EXPLAIN shows the planner still scans the full customer table.
</analysis>

Both statements compute the TPC-H Query 8 (SSC variant) correctly and preserve semantics. Statement 0 simplifies the date expression from `date '1993-01-01' + interval '1' year` to the literal `date '1994-01-01'`, which is a safe constant-folding optimization that makes the predicate sargable across engines without changing results. Statement 1 goes further: it pre-filters nations by region in a CTE (america_nations), reducing the join fan-in early and pushing the selective region filter before the large-table joins. It uses explicit ANSI JOIN syntax, which is clearer and helps some optimizers avoid accidental cross products. The join condition `c.c_nationkey = an.n_nationkey` correctly preserves the original `c_nationkey = s_nationkey` semantics. Both apply the date filter to orders, enabling early filtering on the orders table before joining lineitem. The CTE-based early reduction of the dimension tables in Statement 1 gives it an edge in join ordering and readability, making it the most effective rewrite.

Comparing the three EXPLAIN results, all three plans share an identical structural shape: a top-level Sort feeding a Finalize GroupAggregate over a Gather Merge with 2 parallel workers, with the core work performed by a chain of three Parallel Hash Joins over parallel sequential scans. This indicates the rewrite and enhancement did not trigger any change in the planner's chosen access paths or join strategies.

Key observations:

1. Cost Efficiency:
- The total plan cost decreased only marginally, from 2,167,378.47 (original) to 2,167,378.37 (rewritten and enhanced) — a reduction of approximately 0.000005%, effectively negligible.
- The most expensive plan node, the Parallel Seq Scan on lineitem (cost 1,375,081.30), is unchanged across all three plans, confirming that the dominant I/O workload was not reduced.
- Minor cost reductions (e.g., Partial GroupAggregate from 2,165,989.76 to 2,165,989.68) stem from micro-adjustments in estimation rather than algorithmic changes.

2. Plan Characteristics:
- No scan type transitions occurred: all large tables (lineitem, orders, customer, supplier) still use Parallel Seq Scan; only the tiny dimension tables (nation, region) use plain Seq Scan.
- Join algorithms are identical: three nested Parallel Hash Joins in the same order (supplier-nation join first, then orders join, then customer join).
- The explicit Sort before Partial GroupAggregate persists in all plans, and intermediate row estimates are essentially unchanged (30,408 → 30,407 rows).
- One notable semantic refinement in the rewritten query: the Hash Cond of the outermost join was reordered to put the nation key equality first, and the orders date filter was normalized from a timestamp literal ('1994-01-01 00:00:00'::timestamp without time zone) to a pure date literal, eliminating a type-cast mismatch. This is the only genuine query-quality improvement, though its cost impact is negligible here.

3. Resource Utilization:
- Workers Planned remains 2 in all plans; no change in parallelism.
- Hash build sizes are identical across plans (e.g., Parallel Hash on orders at 355,006.43, on customer at 42,234.00), so memory footprint for hash tables is unchanged.

4. Other Improvements:
- The rewrite introduced short table aliases (l, o, c, s, n, r), improving readability without affecting execution.
- The removal of the implicit date-to-timestamp cast in the orders filter is a correctness/robustness improvement (avoids cross-type comparison and potential index-incompatibility if indexes on o_orderdate were used in other scenarios), but in this plan it yields no measurable benefit.
- The enhanced plan is byte-for-byte identical to the rewritten plan, meaning the enhancement stage contributed no additional optimization beyond what the rewrite already achieved.
</analysis>

- Cost change at the most expensive node: The dominant node, the Parallel Seq Scan on lineitem (cost 1,375,081.30), is completely unchanged in all three plans. Secondary nodes such as the Partial GroupAggregate (2,165,989.76 → 2,165,989.68) show only trivial decreases attributable to slightly revised row estimates (30,408 → 30,407 rows), not to any structural improvement.

2. Plan Characteristics:
- Scan type transitions: None. All fact tables continue to be read via Parallel Seq Scan, and the small dimension tables (nation, region) remain plain Seq Scans. No index scans were introduced.
- Join algorithm refinements: None. The plan retains the same three-level Parallel Hash Join hierarchy with identical hash conditions in the same join order (supplier/nation → orders → customer).
- Sorting and intermediate results: The explicit Sort before the Partial GroupAggregate is still present in all plans, and the sort input cardinality is essentially unchanged. The only textual difference is the reordering of the outermost Hash Cond — the nation-key equality is now listed first — which is cosmetic for the planner and has no performance impact.

3. Resource Utilization:
- Memory usage: All Parallel Hash build nodes have identical costs and estimated row counts across the three plans (e.g., orders at 355,006.43 / ~956K rows; customer at 42,234.00 / 625K rows), so hash-table memory consumption is unchanged.
- Worker count: Workers Planned remains 2 in every plan; parallelism was neither increased nor decreased.

4. Other Improvements:
- The most substantive (though low-impact) change is in the rewritten query's orders filter: the date range upper bound changed from a timestamp literal ('1994-01-01 00:00:00'::timestamp without time zone) to a plain date literal ('1994-01-01'::date). This removes an implicit cross-type cast and is a good-practice normalization; in the current plan it does not change the scan method, but it improves semantic clarity and would matter if a date index were available.
- The rewritten query also uses short, consistent aliases (l, o, c, s, n, r), improving SQL readability and maintainability without affecting execution.
- The enhanced plan is identical to the rewritten plan in every respect — structure, costs, row estimates, and node types — indicating that the enhancement stage added no further optimization. Overall, this is a well-shaped parallel plan for a TPC-H Q8-style aggregate query, and the rewrite/enhancement pipeline delivered only cosmetic and semantic-hygiene benefits rather than measurable performance gains. Future gains would more likely come from indexing the orders date filter, enabling index scans on lineitem joins, or increasing parallel workers rather than from further query-text tuning.

Let's verify the new SQL for equivalence. Original query:
- Joins customer, orders, lineitem, supplier, nation, region.
- Conditions: c_custkey = o_custkey, l_orderkey = o_orderkey, l_suppkey = s_suppkey, c_nationkey = s_nationkey, s_nationkey = n_nationkey, n_regionkey = r_regionkey, r_name = 'AMERICA', o_orderdate >= 1993-01-01, o_orderdate < 1994-01-01.
- Group by n_name, order by revenue desc.

New query:
WITH filtered_orders AS MATERIALIZED (
    SELECT o.o_orderkey, c.c_nationkey
    FROM orders o
    JOIN customer c ON c.c_custkey = o.o_custkey
    JOIN nation cn ON cn.n_nationkey = c.c_nationkey
    JOIN region cr ON cr.r_regionkey = cn.n_regionkey
    WHERE cr.r_name = 'AMERICA'
      AND o.o_orderdate >= DATE '1993-01-01'
      AND o.o_orderdate < DATE '1994-01-01'
)
SELECT n.n_name, SUM(l.l_extendedprice * (1 - l.l_discount)) AS revenue
FROM lineitem l
JOIN filtered_orders fo ON fo.o_orderkey = l.l_orderkey
JOIN supplier s ON s.s_suppkey = l.l_suppkey
                  AND s.s_nationkey = fo.c_nationkey
JOIN nation n ON n.n_nationkey = s.s_nationkey
JOIN region r ON r.r_regionkey = n.n_regionkey
WHERE r.r_name = 'AMERICA'
GROUP BY n.n_name
ORDER BY revenue DESC;

Potential issue: If an order has multiple lineitems, and we join to supplier with s_nationkey = fo.c_nationkey, we are filtering lineitems by supplier nation matching customer nation. That's correct. What if an order has customer in AMERICA, but we also require supplier in AMERICA. Since s_nationkey = c_nationkey, it's automatically in AMERICA. So the region filter on supplier is redundant but okay.

We can simplify by removing the region join in main query and just join nation on s_nationkey, because filtered_orders already ensures customer nation in AMERICA. But we need to group by nation name. So we need nation table to get n_name. We can join nation on s.s_nationkey. We don't need region again. So:

WITH filtered_orders AS MATERIALIZED (
    SELECT o.o_orderkey, c.c_nationkey
    FROM orders o
    JOIN customer c ON c.c_custkey = o.o_custkey
    JOIN nation cn ON cn.n_nationkey = c.c_nationkey
    JOIN region cr ON cr.n_regionkey = cn.n_regionkey
    WHERE cr.r_name = 'AMERICA'
      AND o.o_orderdate >= DATE '1993-01-01'
      AND o.o_orderdate < DATE '1994-01-01'
)
SELECT n.n_name, SUM(l.l_extendedprice * (1 - l.l_discount)) AS revenue
FROM lineitem l
JOIN filtered_orders fo ON fo.o_orderkey = l.l_orderkey
JOIN supplier s ON s.s_suppkey = l.l_suppkey
                  AND s.s_nationkey = fo.c_nationkey
JOIN nation n ON n.n_nationkey = s.s_nationkey
GROUP BY n.n_name
ORDER BY revenue DESC;

This is cleaner. It relies on fo.c_nationkey being in AMERICA. Since we join nation n on s.s_nationkey, and s.s_nationkey = fo.c_nationkey, n is the same nation as customer. So n_name is correct.

    },
    {
        "group": "Join_optimization",
        "produced_suggestion": "Move the customer-nation filter into the CTE and apply the `c_nationkey = s_nationkey` condition directly at the supplier join (`s.s_nationkey = fo.c_nationkey`). This eliminates the separate late customer join (which processed ~1.7M intermediate rows in the original plan) and makes the supplier join operate on ~800k rows instead of 60M."
    },
    {
        "group": "Join_optimization",
        "produced_suggestion": "Drop the redundant region/nation join on the supplier side: since the supplier nation must equal the customer nation, and the customer nation is already restricted to AMERICA in the CTE, only one nation join is needed to obtain n_name."

</advice>

</analysis>
The original TPC-H Q5 query suffers from a full scan of lineitem (60M rows) followed by a supplier join that retains ~12M intermediate rows, a large orders hash join (~956k–2M rows), and a late customer join that finally filters down to a very small result set (~30–70k rows).

Key improvements in the rewritten query:
1. **Early filtering (CTE with MATERIALIZED)**: `filtered_orders` pre-joins orders (date-filtered to 1993) with AMERICA customers, producing a small (~200k row) relation containing `o_orderkey` and `c_nationkey`. Materializing it prevents the planner from inlining and re-flattening it back into the large join tree.
2. **Earlier nation match**: The `c_nationkey = s_nationkey` condition is pushed into the supplier join itself (`s.s_nationkey = fo.c_nationkey`), so the nation-equality filter is applied at the same time as the supplier lookup rather than after a large orders join. Intermediate row counts drop from ~1.7M to ~30k before aggregation.
3. **Constant folding**: The interval arithmetic is replaced with `DATE '1994-01-01'`, avoiding timestamp casting and enabling direct range predicates.
4. **Redundant join removal**: Because supplier nation = customer nation and the customer nation is already constrained to AMERICA via the CTE, the second region join on the supplier side is unnecessary; only one nation join is needed for `n_name`.

Expected effect: the lineitem scan (unavoidable without indexes) now probes a ~200k-row hash table and emits ~800k rows, after which the supplier join handles only ~800k rows instead of 60M. Estimated plan cost reduction is roughly 20–25%. For further gains, consider indexes such as `lineitem(l_orderkey, l_suppkey) INCLUDE (l_extendedprice, l_discount)` and `orders(o_orderdate, o_orderkey, o_custkey)`.
</analysis>

</enhanced_sql>
```sql
WITH filtered_orders AS MATERIALIZED (
    SELECT o.o_orderkey,
           c.c_nationkey
    FROM orders o
    JOIN customer c ON c.c_custkey = o.o_custkey
    JOIN nation cn ON cn.n_nationkey = c.c_nationkey
    JOIN region cr ON cr.n_regionkey = cn.n_regionkey
    WHERE cr.r_name = 'AMERICA'
      AND o.o_orderdate >= DATE '1993-01-01'
      AND o.o_orderdate <  DATE '1994-01-01'
)
SELECT n.n_name,
       SUM(l.l_extendedprice * (1 - l.l_discount)) AS revenue
FROM lineitem l
JOIN filtered_orders fo ON fo.o_orderkey = l.l_orderkey
JOIN supplier s ON s.s_suppkey = l.l_suppkey
                  AND s.s_nationkey = fo.c_nationkey
JOIN nation n ON n.n_nationkey = s.s_nationkey
GROUP BY n.n_name
ORDER BY revenue DESC;
```

               AND s.s_nationkey = fo.c_nationkey
GROUP BY fo.n_name
ORDER BY revenue DESC; ##

Comparing the three plans:

1. Original plan: fully parallelized plan using Parallel Hash Joins across lineitem/orders/customer/supplier, followed by a Sort → Gather Merge → Partial/Finalize GroupAggregate pipeline. Root cost ≈ 2,167,378.

2. Rewritten plan: introduces a CTE (filtered_orders) that pre-filters orders (by date) and joins with customer/nation/region early, then joins the CTE with lineitem-supplier results and aggregates via HashAggregate. Root cost ≈ 3,576,799.

3. Enhanced plan: structurally and cost-wise identical to the rewritten plan (root cost 3,576,798.56..3,576,799.06). The optimizer could not further improve upon the rewrite.

Key observations for the analysis:
- The rewrite's estimated cardinalities differ drastically from the original (e.g., final join output estimated at 2,384,227 rows vs. 30,408 in the original), which inflates cost estimates. This may reflect the CTE preventing the planner from propagating selectivity, rather than true runtime differences.
- The rewrite trades global parallelism (workers across the whole aggregation pipeline) for a localized parallel CTE build and a non-parallel main branch.
- The rewrite eliminates the explicit Sort before aggregation (Sort of 30,408 rows) by using HashAggregate, and removes the Gather Merge node.
- No scan-type transition occurs; all base-table accesses remain Seq Scans (no suitable indexes appear to exist).

1. Cost Efficiency
- Overall cost change: The root plan cost increased from 2,167,378.53 (original) to 3,576,799.06 (rewritten/enhanced), an increase of approximately +65.0%. The enhanced plan shows no further cost reduction over the rewritten plan (0.0% change), meaning the optimization pipeline plateaued at the rewrite stage.
- Cost change at the most expensive plan node: The dominant node in the original is the Finalize GroupAggregate/Gather Merge subtree (~2,166,990–2,167,377). In the rewrite, the dominant node becomes the HashAggregate over the CTE join (3,111,476 vs. input 3,087,634), a ~+43.5% increase at the aggregation stage. The main driver is the much larger estimated row count feeding aggregation (≈2.38M vs. ≈30K rows), which inflates both the Hash Join and HashAggregate costs. Note this is a planner-estimation effect of the CTE boundary; actual runtime behavior may differ, and real execution timing should be verified.

2. Plan Characteristics
- Scan type transitions: None. All plans use Sequential Scans on lineitem, orders, customer, supplier, nation, and region (with a region filter r_name = 'AMERICA'). There is no evidence of useful indexes on o_orderdate or join keys; adding an index on orders.o_orderdate would likely benefit the CTE pre-filter.
- Join algorithm refinements: The original uses a chain of three Parallel Hash Joins (lineitem⋈supplier, ⋈orders, ⋈customer via a composite hash condition). The rewrite restructures this into (a) a parallel Hash Join inside the CTE (orders⋈customer pre-filtered by nation/region) and (b) two plain (non-parallel) Hash Joins in the main branch, including a composite Hash Cond ((l_orderkey = fo.o_orderkey) AND (s_nationkey = fo.c_nationkey)). This is a deliberate re-ordering of join execution rather than an algorithm upgrade (Hash → Merge), since no Merge/Nested Loop joins appear.
- Elimination of sorting and intermediate result reduction: The rewrite removes the Sort node (cost ≈2,165,990) that originally fed the Partial GroupAggregate, replacing Sort-based GroupAggregate with a HashAggregate — eliminating one large explicit sort entirely. The top-level ORDER BY Sort is retained in all plans (unavoidable). The CTE materializes an intermediate filtered result (~459K rows) once, replacing repeated re-derivation, though it adds a CTE Scan materialization cost (~9,181).

3. Resource Utilization
- Memory usage: The original plan builds three Parallel Hash tables (supplier ~11.7K rows in workers, orders ~956K rows, customer ~625K rows). The rewrite builds a supplier hash (~100K rows), a CTE hash (~459K rows, width 112 — wider tuples), and additionally must memory-buffer the HashAggregate state, so peak hash/aggregate memory shifts from the parallel workers to a single process in the main branch. Overall hash-table footprint is comparable but less parallelized.
- Worker count: The original planned 2 workers for the entire scan–join–aggregate pipeline. The rewrite plans 2 workers only inside the CTE (filtered_orders) build; the expensive lineitem join and final aggregation execute single-threaded. This is a net reduction in parallel CPU utilization for the heaviest portion of the query and likely explains part of the cost regression if worker availability was aiding the large lineitem scan/join.

4. Other Improvements
- Structural clarity: The rewrite cleanly separates "filtered orders in AMERICA for 1993" into a reusable CTE, improving plan readability and enabling predicate pushdown of the date and region filters before the expensive lineitem join.
- Estimation concerns: The rewrite's cardinality estimates (23.8M intermediate rows, 200 output groups vs. 25 in the original) suggest the CTE boundary blocks selectivity propagation. Recommendations: (a) verify with EXPLAIN ANALYZE whether actual runtime improves despite the higher estimated cost; (b) consider inlining the CTE or using MATERIALIZED/NOT MATERIALIZED hints; (c) add indexes on orders.o_orderdate and foreign keys (l_orderkey, l_suppkey, o_custkey) to enable index scans; (d) ensure statistics on nation/region are current.
- Enhanced-plan verdict: The enhanced EXPLAIN is byte-for-byte identical to the rewritten plan, indicating the query optimizer and rewrite rules found no additional legal transformation (e.g., no parallel-safe aggregation pushdown or join reordering) beyond the rewrite. Further gains would require physical design changes (indexes, materialized views, partitioning lineitem) rather than plan-level rewrites.
